At the worked thing standing in the door with its back turned.

And Toren had been there.

He was standing a pace behind Callie on the room side with his left arm in its cuff against his chest. There was a strut going up from the concrete beside his boot at a slant, over Callie's shoulders, the way you lean a plank over a door. The arm-rope lay across it. It had hit the strut at the height of Callie's head and stopped there, draped over the pale line of it, and it was sliding off slowly, the way a wet rope slides off a rail.

"Holding," said Toren. His teeth were together.

Callie had not turned round. Jab saw that. He saw her shoulders move at the sound of it hitting, and not turn.

The rope slid off the strut onto the floor and lay there.

"Letting go," said Toren, and the strut went.

"Husks," said Dee. "Two."

The corner heap had stood up. So had the heap by the cabinets, at the far end of the row, three cabinets from where the frame was. Two husks, grey, faceless, the colour of old wasp nests. They turned toward the worked things in the room. Toren's heel, still lit a hand's width. Callie's back. Jab's palm, which had been worked a breath ago and was open now.

"I've got the cabinet one," said Toren.

He went at it. He did not go at it with the heel. He set a strut from the floor in front of it, slanting back toward the cabinets, and it walked into the strut and stood against it, pushing, pinned between the pale line and the steel of the cabinet door behind it. He stood behind the strut, soft, paying by the breath.

"And the corner one?" said Dee.

It was coming along the boxes toward Jab.

---

He let the knife come.

It came on his left fist, the way it always came, dark, heavy in the spine, fat in the belly. The fish-knife. The one shape he had lived inside for four years. He did not light it all. He lit the last inch of it, the hook, and felt the draw of it behind his breastbone.

The husk came along the heap of boxes at him, low.

He did not want to kill it. Every one you kill is lying on the floor. And then it's in the air. And then at the three it's standing up again. He knew that. He had watched it happen five times yesterday. But it was coming at his hands, and Toren was on the strut, and Dee had the jar, and Callie had the door.

He killed it.

He put the hook into it low as it came, the way he would have cut a rope, and it came apart. The grey fell on the boxes and the floor at the foot of the racks in a heap.

"A hundred and twenty," said Dee, with the counter.

Jab let the knife go dark.

He crouched by the heap. He did not lift it. There was no time to lift it and he knew what lifting against the lifter was like. He watched it. Ten breaths. It drew out into threads and went into the strands at the foot of the racks and along them into the corner. Into the heap the husk had stood up out of.

He went after it. He went along the foot of the racks, crouching, with his right hand out, to the corner. He put his left hand up behind him without looking and Dee was there with the jar and put its mouth against his palm. She had followed him.

"Three," said Dee.

The corner swelled.

He put his hand flat on it. On the top of the heap, where it swelled, where the husk was going to stand.

It pushed into him.

It was the same. A push. A palm's worth, warm, gritty, loose, all at once, and then nothing behind it. He held it high. The heap under his hand did not crack. It did not stand. It sank back a little, like a loaf that has been knocked.

"Off," said Dee.

He was already off.

The vent jar. The grey going down into the glass. The stopper.

"Two," said Dee.

The corner heap lay in the corner. It was smaller than it had been. It did not stand up.

---

"It doesn't matter where," said Jab.

He said it to Dee, crouching in the corner with his hand held away from him. The lantern was behind him on the cabinet. Dee's face was yellow on one side.

"It doesn't matter where I take it," he said. "The cut. The corner. Wherever it pushes. It's all out of the drum." He looked at the hump over the carrier. "Every time I take one, there's a palm's worth less of it. Anywhere."

"Then take it where it matters," said Dee.

She said it at once. She had known it before he said it, he thought. She had been waiting for him to find it.

"Where it's going to stand up," said Jab. "Where it's mending something Toren did. Where it's going to hurt somebody." He looked at Toren on the strut with the husk against it. "There."

"Then that's the order," said Dee. "You'll not get all of them. It pushes into every hole at once. You'll get one." She looked at the glass in her fist. "Pick the one."

---

It learned.

Jab felt it learn. He did not know how else to say it afterward, and he did not try to. It had nobody in it. It did not want anything. But it did what it did, and when what it did stopped working, it did something else. The way water does.

At the next three it did not push into the corner.

It pushed into the husk on Toren's strut.

He felt it go. It went out of the hump across the floor along the strands, not to the corner, but to the thing pinned against the cabinet at the end of the row. He saw the husk get bigger. It swelled against the strut like a sack being filled, taller, thicker, and the strut took more of it. He heard Toren make a noise.

"It's feeding it," said Jab. "The one on your strut."

"I can feel it's feeding it," said Toren. "It's heavier."

Jab went. He went along the cabinets fast, crouching, with his hand out behind him for the jar. But the three was over before he got there. The ten had begun. The husk on the strut was as big as a man now, and it leaned.

"Stone's half," said Toren. "I'm paying for that by the breath. It's more than a wheel."

"Next three," said Jab. "I'll be on it."

"On what? On the husk?"

"On whatever it pushes into."

He crouched at the foot of the strut, at the husk's feet, with his right hand an inch off its grey where it stood on the floor. He did not touch it. You did not touch a husk. But this was not a husk now, not only. It was a place the lifter pushed into. It was a hole in the lifter, walking about, and the lifter was trying to fill it.

"Clean in," said Dee, behind him, with the jar at his left palm.

"Three," said Jab, before she did.

It pushed. He put his palm flat on the foot of the husk, where it stood on the concrete.

It came into him instead.

He felt it come. It came along the floor from the hump and up into the husk and, at the husk's foot, it found his palm drawing, and came into it. Warm. A palm's worth. The husk did not grow. It shook, against the strut, the way a sack shakes when you pull something out of the bottom of it.

"Off," said Jab.

He came off. The vent jar. The grey. The stopper.

"Three," said Dee.

The husk leaned on the strut. It was as big as it had been, and no bigger.

"It's not heavier," said Toren. "It's not getting heavier."

---

Toren began to cut.

He did it on the tens. He left the husk on its strut, pinned, and went to the hump.

The arm-ropes lay out from it across the boxes and the floor. There had been three. One lay where it had slid off the strut behind Callie, on the floor near the door, across the room from the hump. The other two lay across the boxes, out from the far side of the hump, toward the corner.

He went to the near one first. It lay on the floor between the hump and the door. He lit the heel. He put it across the arm-rope a pace from the hump, low, and dragged it through.

It came apart. The long end lay on the floor toward the door, spilling. The short end stuck out of the hump like the stump of a branch.

"Cut," said Toren. "Near one."

"Three in four," said Dee.

Jab went to the stump.

He was learning the room. He did not know that he was until he was doing it. Where the lantern light fell and where it did not. Where the boxes were, and the carrier under the hump with its iron handle sticking out, and the cabinets. Where Callie's heels were, and the curve on the threshold, and Toren. He went round all of it at a crouch with his right hand out in front of him and his left hand back, and Dee came behind him with the jar and the bag. She was always there when he put his hand back.

He put his palm an inch off the stump.

"Two," said Dee. "Three."

It pushed. The stump grew at him, the way the cut strands had grown toward each other on the floor yesterday, a finger, two fingers, to mend. He put his palm on the end of it. It came into him instead of growing.

"Off."

The jar. The grey. The stopper.

"Four," said Dee. "That's the first jar full. Near enough." She put it in the bag. "Two left."

The stump stuck out of the hump, short, a hand long. It did not grow. The long end of the arm-rope lay on the floor by the door with its spill going away from it in threads.

Toren looked at it.

"That's one," he said. "It's got two."

---

It learned again.

At the next three it threw.

It did not push into a hole. It lifted the second arm-rope, the one across the boxes toward the corner, and swung it. It swung it at Jab.

He did not see it come. He had his hand an inch off the stump of the first arm, and his eyes on it, and Dee's jar at his left palm, and his attention down under his palm in the grey. He heard the long heavy dry sound of the rope coming up off the boxes. He heard Toren say "*Jab*."

Something went over his head.

It went over him the way a roof goes over you. Pale, and hard, a line of light going up from the concrete at a slant beside his knee and on over the top of him and Dee, like a plank leaned against a wall with the two of them under it. And the arm-rope hit it.

He felt it hit. He felt it through the floor, through his boots, through his knees on the concrete. It was the sound of a sack hitting a wall. The strut took it. It stood over him and Dee, pale, and the rope lay over the top of it and began to slide.

"Holding," said Toren. "Both."

Jab looked round.

Toren was standing between the hump and the cabinets with his left arm against his chest. He had two struts up. One went from the floor to the husk at the end of the cabinets, pinning it. The other went from the floor over Jab and Dee. He was soft behind both of them. His face was grey and wet in the lantern light.

He had held two at once on the arch at the wash, for as long as it took to walk a cart onto a post. Jab had heard him say it. *Two struts while it moves. That's twice the price.*

"Toren," said Jab.

"Get the stump," said Toren. "I've got the roof."

The arm-rope slid off the strut onto the boxes. The ten had begun.

---

Jab put his hand on the stump of the first arm at the next three and it did not push. The lifter pushed at the second arm instead, the one that had hit the roof, to mend where the strut had broken it. The rope was cracked across where it had struck, and the lifter was mending it.

He felt it go the wrong way. He felt it go past him.

"It's gone to the other," he said.

"Then leave it," said Dee.

"It'll swing again."

"Then Toren'll roof you again." She did not raise her voice. "You can't be on all of them. It pushes into every hole at once. You're on one. Pick the one."

"I picked the wrong one."

"Then pick the next one," said Dee. "That's all a watch is."

---

He came off late once.

It was the seventh. He had his palm on the corner heap, which the lifter was trying to stand a husk up out of again. The push came into his hand, a palm's worth. He held it high. He was thinking about the second arm-rope, which was lying across the boxes behind him, and about where it would swing. He was thinking about Toren, with his face grey. He was thinking about Callie's back.

"Off," said Dee.

He was not off.

The ten began.

He felt it begin under his palm. He felt the lifter draw. It drew the way it had drawn all afternoon yesterday, flat and open and patient, at everything loose in the room. And the palm's worth in his hand was loose. It was held high and open in his palm, and it was not his, and it was not in a jar.

It came out of his hand.

He felt it go. It went out of his palm back down into the corner heap in threads, the way the heap on the floor had gone out from under his palm yesterday. And his palm went with it. Not the hand. The drawing. The part of him that was reaching down into the grey went with the threads, toward the hump, toward the middle, the way you lean after a rope that is being pulled away from you.

For a breath he was going.

"*Off*," said Dee.

He took his hand off.

He knelt in the corner with his palm up and empty. Half the palm's worth had gone back into the heap. He could feel where it had gone. He could feel where he had nearly gone after it.

"Off before the ten," said Dee. "Not at the ten. Before."

"I know."

"You know now," said Dee.

He looked at his palm. Round the white place, the rest of it was warm, a little. Not lodged. Warm.

"Vent," said Dee, and held the jar.

He vented the half into the glass. She put the stopper in.

"Seven," said Dee. "Half of it."

He did not come off late again. At every three after that he said *off* himself, before she did, and was off, the way he had said *vent* before she did at the mouth of the cut. And every time, as he lifted his palm, he felt the ten begin a breath behind it. It was like stepping off a step just as it gives.

---

He looked at Callie once.

He could not help it. He was crouched by the stump of the first arm-rope waiting for a three, near the door, and she was there, a pace and a half from him, with her back to him.

He had not looked at her properly since they came in. He looked now.

She was standing in the doorway with the curve on the threshold and her hands on the conduit. The lee stood up out of the curve on the corridor side, and he could see it past her. It was a pale sheet standing up off the threshold and leaning back up the wall over the top of the door, like a lean-to the wrong way up. It was grey on the outside. The grey of the corridor's cut rope had heaped against its foot in a long ridge, as high as Callie's knees now, piled against the outside of the pale wall. And the ceiling rope's cut end had heaped against it up by the hole.

And there was a husk against it.

It stood in the corridor on the other side of the lee, pushing. It had come from the stair door. It had come at the worked thing in the doorway and walked into the lee and stood there, pressed against the pale wall a foot from Callie's face, pushing, with no face of its own. Every time it pushed, he saw Callie's shoulders take it.

She was holding it off her stone. Her whole stone. Two Handfuls, and the husk on the outside, and the heaps, and a door and a bit.

There was blood on her lip.

She turned her head.

She did not turn round. She turned her head, a little, over her shoulder, the way you turn your head at a noise behind you. And for a breath her eyes found him, crouched on the floor with his hand out over a stump, and Dee behind him with the jar, and the room behind that.

He did not know what she saw. He did not know what her face did. He looked at her and said the only thing he had.

"Busy," he said.

Callie looked at him a breath longer.

Then she turned her head back to the corridor. She did not turn it again.

---

"Jar," said Jab, at the tenth.

He said it before it was dry. He felt the clean cold coming thin into his left palm, the pour at the bottom of the jug.

Dee did not answer for a moment.

"That's two done," she said. "The open one's done."

She turned. The frame was on the cabinet top behind them. Jab saw her hand go to it. There were two full jars in it now, and two empties, and the broken socket. And the jar that was empty in her arm.

She stopped with her hand on the neck of the third.

"That's the last one above the line," she said.

Nobody said anything. Toren was on his two struts, the husk and the roof, with his face running wet. Callie was in the door. The lifter lay over the carrier.

"Open it," said Toren.

"It's the last above," said Dee again. "When it's done, it's the line. I'm saying it now so it's said."

"It's said," said Toren. "Open it."

She opened it. She lifted it out of the frame by the neck and pulled the stopper and put the mouth of it against Jab's left palm.

"Three," she said. "Open. Two whole. And that's sixteen, in the two." She looked at the hump. "Say it, Jab."

"Eight above the line," said Jab. "In this one. Then it's the line."

---

It was smaller.

Jab saw it as he went round the hump for the eleventh. He had been too near it to see it, the way you do not see a heap of sacks going down while you are lifting off the top of it. The hump had been as high as Callie's waist. It was as high as her knee now, at the high end. The other end had gone down to nothing, and he could see the carrier's lead lid under it, grey, and the iron handle all the way out.

"It's smaller," he said.

"It's less," said Toren. "I can feel it on the husk. The husk's less."

It was. The husk on Toren's strut had been as big as a man. It was not now. It was the size of a boy, pinned against the cabinet, and it pushed with less.

And the three was different.

He felt it before he knew what it was. The line of light opened along the top of the hump at the three, the colour of the edge of the sky. And it stayed open. It stayed open longer. It opened at the three and it did not shut at the three. It shut at the end of four. Then five.

"It's pushing longer," said Jab.

"Why?"

"Because there's less in the push." He did not know how he knew. He knew it the way he knew a floor. "It's pushing everything it lifted, and it didn't lift much. So it keeps pushing. Looking for more." He looked at the light. "It's opening wider because it's got nothing to put through."

"Pumping itself dry," said Dee.

"It doesn't know how to stop," said Jab.

He crouched by the stump of the first arm-rope with his hand out. He thought about it, while the ten ran. He thought about the rope by the door yesterday, and the push that would not stop, and the whole of the hill behind it. And himself, with his palm on it, not able to find his floor.

It could not find its floor either. There was nobody in it to find it. And there was nobody in it to say *off*.

The difference between him and it was one word, and Dee.

---

The lifter moved.

It had not moved since they came in. It had lain over the carrier like a dog over a bone. It had thrown its arm-ropes and pushed into its holes and stood its husks up, and it had not moved.

At the fourteenth three it moved.

It pulled itself. It did it the way it had come along the corridor yesterday, drawing itself along its own rope. But the only rope it had now that went anywhere was the cut end of the big one, the stump that ran from the hump across the floor to the threshold. To the curve of the hook. To Callie's heels.

It came along it.

It came off the carrier. It slid off the lead lid and along the floor toward the door, the whole grey hump of it, low, knee-high, pulling itself along the stump the way a man pulls himself along a rope in a flood. Toward the threshold. Toward the lee. Toward the river on the other side of it.

"It's going for the door," said Jab.

Toren saw it. Jab saw him see it. He had two struts up, the husk and the roof over Jab, and he let the roof go, because Jab was not under it now. He put the foot of it down on the floor in front of the hump, a pace short of Callie's heels.

The lifter came against the strut.

It leaned on it. It came up against the pale line of it, low, the whole grey weight, and leaned, and did not push the way a husk pushed. It leaned the way the cart had leaned on the arch. Jab saw it in Toren's face.

"*Holding*," said Toren. And then, not to anybody: "That's the lean. That's the cart."

"Toren—"

"I've got it," said Toren. "Stone's gone. I'm on mine." He did not move. "I've got a turn of mine. And a bit. Get it, Jab. Whatever it's pushing, get it."

---

"Jar," said Jab.

He heard himself say it. It was the eighteenth three, or the nineteenth. He had lost count. He had not lost count of the jar. He felt it go thin.

Dee had it against his palm. She held it there a breath longer than she needed to, as if she had not heard.

Then she took it away.

"That's the line," she said.

It went round the room. Jab felt it go. Toren on his two struts, one on the lifter, one on the husk, with his face grey and running and his own reserve going under both of them by the breath. Callie in the door with her back to them, blood on her lip, holding. Dee with the empty jar in her hand and the glass in the other. And himself, on his knees by the lifter with his palm an inch off its edge.

"Sixteen in the frame," said Dee. "Two whole. That's the line. Everybody out."

Nobody moved.

"If we go," said Toren, "it's at the door. It's against my strut. When I let go, it goes against the lee. When Callie lets go of the lee, it's through. It's in the corridor with the rope. It's got the river."

"I know where it is," said Dee.

"It's got nothing," said Toren. "Look at it. It's knee-high. It's opening five breaths. It's got nothing left to push but itself."

"I know what it's got," said Dee. "I've been counting it into jars."

She did not look at Toren. She looked at Jab.

"What do you feel?" she said.

He knew what she was asking. It was not the counter. It was not the jar. It was Rook's question, the one Rook had asked at the ditch and at the pen with his hand on Jab's shoulder doing nothing. *What's there?* She was asking it of him. She was asking him what she could not measure.

Jab put his palm an inch off the edge of the lifter, where it leaned on Toren's strut. He shut his eyes.

It was there. Grey, loose, nobody in it. It was drawing, in the ten, on what little was loose in the room. The spill of the cut arms, and the husk's grey, and the ridge along the foot of the racks. It was drawing at the rate it came and it was not coming, because there was so little left lying anywhere in the room. It was drawing on nearly nothing.

And under the draw, at the middle of it, where the light opened at the three, there was a place. It was a hole. It was not a hole anybody had made. It was the place the push came from, the vent, and it was open wider every time. It was a hole in the middle of the lifter that the lifter was pushing out of, and it had nearly nothing left to push.

"It's missing," he said. "The three. It's pushing, and nothing's coming back in the ten. It's nearly dry." He opened his eyes. "One more, maybe. Two. Then it'll open, and there'll be nothing to shut it with."

Dee looked at him for a long moment.

Then she looked at the counter on her shoulder.

"A hundred and thirty," she said. "Not red."

Then at Toren.

"Say it," she said.

---

Toren said it.

He said it from behind his two struts, with the lifter leaning on one and the husk on the other and his own reserve going under both. He said it with his teeth together, flat, in order, the way Pell said a distance.

"We cross it," he said. "The line. Now. On purpose. Two turns of the glass. Not three. If it's not open for the point in two turns, everybody out, finished or not, and it has the door." He breathed. "It's mine. I'm saying it. Write it."

"It's mine as well," said Dee. "It's my line." She did not write it. She had no hand for the book. She said it instead, out loud, to the room, so it was said. "Two turns under the line. His call and mine. Written tonight." She turned the glass in her fist. "The glass is running."

She reached back to the frame on the cabinet. She took the fourth jar out by its neck, one of the last two whole ones. She pulled the stopper. She put it against Jab's left palm.

"Four," she said. "Open. One whole. That's under."

Jab felt the clean cold come in.

It was the fence's. It had been for the stair, and the rooms, and the road home. It was under the line, where it was never supposed to be opened except to get somebody out. He knew that. He knew what it cost. Every Handful that came out of this jar was a Handful nobody had.

He felt it come in at his left palm and go through him and wait.

"Callie," said Toren. "Two turns."

"I heard," said Callie, from the door. She did not turn her head. Her voice was flat and thick, the way it was with a nosebleed. "I've got two turns. I've not got three."

---

At the next three the light opened, and stayed.

It opened along the top of the lifter where it leaned on Toren's strut, the colour of the bottom of a pond. It opened wide. Jab could see into it. There was nothing in it. There was only the light, green-grey, pushing out, like breath out of a mouth, with nothing behind it.

It pushed at every hole in the room at once. He felt it go. At the stumps. At the corner. At the husk on the strut, which shivered. At the cut arm by the door.

He did not go to any of them.

He put his palm on the lifter.

He put it flat on the edge of the grey where it leaned against Toren's strut, a hand from where the light opened. He had never put it on the lifter itself. He had been on its ropes and its holes and its stumps all morning. He put it on the thing now.

It was not warm. That was the first thing. It had been warm all yesterday, all through, the whole of the hill in it. It was not warm now. It was grey, and loose, and cool, and thin, like flour in the bottom of a sack.

It pushed into him.

It pushed into him because he was drawing, and he was a hole, and it had never known anything else to do with a hole. It pushed what it had. It was very little.

He held it high. He did not let it into his wrist. He held it there, in his palm, the last of what it had, and felt it go on pushing, five breaths, six, with nothing, into his hand. Like breath going out of a man who has no more breath.

*Off before the ten.*

He did not take his hand off.

Not yet. He knew he had to. He knew the ten was coming a breath behind the three, and that it would draw, and that what he had in his palm was loose, and it would draw it back, and him after it. He knew all of that.

But the light was open. It was open wide, a hand from his palm. And it was open because he was on it, taking the push, so there was nothing to shut it with. If he came off now, the ten would come, and it would draw what little was lying in the room, and at the next three it would have something to push, and it would shut.

"Toren," he said.

Toren was there.

He had let the husk go. Jab heard the strut go out and the husk fall forward onto the floor by the cabinets and not get up. It did not get up, because there was nothing coming to it. Toren was standing over the lifter with his one strut still under it, soft, and the spike on his fist, dark.

"It's open," said Jab. "It's open and I'm on it. It's got nothing to shut it with."

"How long?"

"Till the ten," said Jab. "Then I'm off. Whatever you've done."

Toren lit the spike.

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-02-meridian/manuscript/chapter-39.md

# Chapter 39 — Toren: The Point

Toren lit the point.

He did not light the heel. He had nothing to light the heel with and the point both, and the heel was for hooking, and there was nothing to hook. He asked for the light at the very end of the spike, a finger's length of it, and only there. He asked it of his own, because the stone was gone. He felt the draw behind his breastbone, thin, like the last of the water going out of a barrel through a pinhole.

It lit. A finger of white-grey at the end of the ugly crooked thing on his fist.

The lifter lay against his strut, knee-high, grey. The strut went down from its side into the concrete at a slant, and he was soft behind it, and he was paying for it by the breath out of what was left of his own. Beside it, on his knees, Jab had his right palm flat on the edge of the grey, a hand from the light.

The light was open.

It was a line along the top of the lifter, the colour of the bottom of a pond, wide now, as wide as two fingers. It had opened at the three and it had not shut, because it had nothing to shut with. Jab was taking what came out of it. Toren could see Jab's palm, held flat, and his face, and the face was very still. It was the face he had seen on the step of eleven when Jab put his hand on a drift in his sleep.

Everything was where it was.

Callie was in the door behind him with her back to the room and the lee standing out into the corridor off the curve. He could not see the lee from here. He could see her shoulders, and they had not moved. Dee was at Jab's back with the fourth jar against his left palm, under the line, and the glass running in her other hand. The husk he had let go was lying on the floor by the cabinets and not getting up.

He could see all of them. He had been able to see all of them all morning. It was the only thing he had been able to do the whole time that was not holding.

"Six," said Dee, very low. "Seven."

The ten was coming. Jab would take his hand off at the ten. He had said so.

Toren put the point in.

---

He did not stab. He put it in the way you put a knife into a loaf you are cutting at the table, the point first, down, into the line of light, a hand from Jab's palm. He put it in slowly because there was nothing to be quick for. There was no hide and no bone. It was only grey, and light.

The point went into the light.

The light went out.

It went out the way a lamp goes out when you pinch the wick, all at once, with nothing after it. There was a sound. Toren did not know afterward whether it had been a sound. It was more like the air in the room changing, the way the air in a house changes when somebody who was in it has gone out and shut the door.

Then the lifter came apart.

It came apart the way husks came apart, all at once, into grey. But it was not a husk. It was all of it. The whole long low hump of it went, from the end that lay against his strut back to where it had lain over the carrier, and it did not burst, because there was no wind. It fell. It fell in on itself like a heap of flour when you take away the board that was holding it up, and spread out across the floor, over the carrier and the boxes and the concrete and Jab's knees and Toren's boots. A long low slope of grey, knee-deep in the middle, loose.

His strut had nothing on it.

He let it go. At the end. Not after.

"Off," said Jab. He had taken his hand away. He held it up, open, with a palm's worth of grey in it, and turned, and Dee had the last vent jar out, and he tipped it in. "It's off."

"Ten," said Dee.

Toren counted.

Ten breaths. Nothing moved. The grey lay on the floor where it had fallen and did not draw out into threads. It did not go anywhere. It lay there, loose, and was only grey.

Three.

Nothing.

---

He looked at the ropes.

The stumps of the arm-ropes had been sticking out of the hump. They were gone. They had fallen with it. The long end of the first arm-rope lay by the door where he had cut it, and it was not a rope now. It was a line of grey on the concrete, the shape of a rope, lying there, the way a snake's skin lies. The strands at the foot of the racks were lines of grey. The rope that came down the wall from the hole was a streak of grey down the concrete.

"The corridor," he said. "Callie. The corridor."

Callie did not turn round. She did not need to. She was looking at it.

"It's gone," she said. Her voice was thick and flat. "The big one. It's down. It's not a rope. It's a bank of grey along the wall." She was quiet. "The tray's full of grey. It's not going anywhere. The ridge against the lee isn't going anywhere." She breathed. "The husk's come apart. The one on the outside. It just came apart. Nobody touched it."

"They were its hands," said Jab, on his knees.

Nobody answered him.

Toren looked at the floor of the room. At the long slope of grey that had been the lifter. At the carrier's iron handle sticking up out of it.

"It's dead," he said. He heard himself say it. He did not know if that was the word. There had been nobody in it to die. "It's stopped."

"It's stopped," said Dee.

---

"I'm at my floor," said Callie.

She said it from the door, flat, with her back to them.

"I'm at my floor. I'm stopping."

Toren turned.

He saw her let it go. He saw the pale of the curve on the threshold go grey and ordinary, and beyond it, in the corridor, the lee go out. The ridge of grey that had been heaped against its outside slid over the threshold into the room in a slow soft spill and lay there.

And he saw her legs go.

They went the way they had gone at the mouth of the cut at the wash, all at once, without asking her. But she was not standing at the mouth of a cut with a wall behind her. She was standing in a doorway with the hook in her hands and the conduit going down in front of her to the curve on the threshold. The last of the Ward was still in the steel when her knees went. It was still fixed.

She went forward onto it.

She went down onto the conduit of her own hook, with the steel still fixed in the air in front of her like an iron bar set in a wall. It took her across the ribs on the left side, the side she had walked into it on the forty-fourth. He heard her make a sound. It was not loud.

Then the Ward went out of the steel, because she had nothing left to keep it there, and the hook went down onto the threshold with a clang, and she went down on her knees beside it with her arms across her side.

"Callie."

"Don't," said Callie. She was on her knees. Nobody sits down inside the fence. She had not sat. "Don't touch it." She breathed out through her teeth, carefully, the way you let a rope out. "That's cracked."

"Same place?" said Dee. She had not moved from Jab's back. She could not.

"Same place," said Callie. "It's cracked this time. I heard it."

---

Dee measured them where they were.

She did it standing, because she would not kneel, with two fingers on each wrist in turn. She did Jab first. She did Callie on her knees in the doorway, with Callie's arms still across her side. She did Toren.

"Your own's at your floor," she said to Toren. "Near enough. A hair over. You've nothing to light with."

"I know."

"You held two struts and a husk and that thing on the strut," said Dee. "On your own, the last of it. I watched you do it."

She did not say *that was right*. Toren did not need her to.

"Callie's at her floor," said Dee. "Not under. Jab's a hand. His floor's where it was." She stopped.

She had not done her own.

She did it now. She put two fingers on the inside of her own left wrist, the crooked one, and looked at nothing.

Toren watched her face. He had watched it all morning, over the jar, over the glass, over Jab's back. It had been white and still and absolutely steady. It was still steady.

"I'm at my floor," said Dee.

Nobody said anything.

"I've been the watch," said Dee. "The whole time. Every time he came off, I took his hands. Nineteen times. There's nobody to take mine." She took her fingers off her wrist. "My stone went at the tenth, near enough. I've been on my own since then."

"A healer at their floor," said Jab.

"Once," said Dee. "That's once. Twice, and it's everybody out." She looked at the glass in her fist. "And a healer lost, and it's everybody out that moment. I'm not lost. I'm at my floor." She looked at Jab. "But I'll not be drawing anybody. Not here. Not today. You're the only healer in this room now. And nobody can draw you."

"Then we go," said Toren.

"We go," said Dee. "In one working turn. Twenty of the glass, from now." She turned it. "That's the price of the box, and whatever's on this floor. Twenty. And then everybody out, finished or not."

---

"The box," said Toren.

He went to the carrier.

He did not go through the grey. He went round it, along the foot of the cabinets, to where the iron handle stuck up out of the slope. He crouched. He could see the lead lid under the grey, in its groove. He could see where the grey lay over it a hand deep.

"Jab," he said.

"I know," said Jab.

He came along the cabinets on his knees, with his right hand held out. He had not got up since the lifter came apart. Toren did not think he had noticed.

"It's cold," said Jab.

He had put his palm an inch off the grey on the carrier's lid, the way he put it over a patient. He had his eyes shut.

"It's cold," he said again. "Most of it. It's the cold with nobody in it." He moved his hand along, an inch off. "There's warm in it. Grit. The hill. Here, and here. Not much." He opened his eyes. "Most of it's clean. It pushed the hill out at us all morning. What it had left was what it was made of."

"How much?" said Dee.

Jab looked along the slope. From the carrier to the threshold, from the boxes to the middle of the floor, knee-deep in the middle, thinning out.

"More than I've ever seen," he said. "In one place."

"More than we can lift," said Dee.

"More than we can lift in twenty," said Jab.

---

They lifted.

There was no other word for it, and Toren would not have used one. It was the east track, and it was Rook's drift, and it was the old north road on Callie's first morning with her grandfather's flat palm. It was the most ordinary work any of them knew, done on the floor of a room under a hill beside a thing that had tried to fill Jab's hand with the whole of the world.

The empties were in the frame on the cabinet top. The three fence jars that had been opened that morning and yesterday, and the two that had been empty since the cut at the wash. Five. Dee lifted them out one at a time and pulled the stoppers and stood them on the cabinet top in a row.

"Nobody kneels," said Dee. "You crouch. You don't put a knee down."

"I'm on my knees," said Callie, from the door.

"You're hurt," said Dee. "You're allowed your knees. You're not allowed to lift."

"I can lift," said Callie.

She came across the floor on her knees with one arm across her side. She came to the edge of the slope of grey by the door, where the ridge had spilled in over the threshold, and she put her right hand flat on it.

"I've been lifting since I was eleven," she said. "Off husk lines, in the silt. Lifting's what I'm for." She did not look at Dee. "It doesn't come out of me. It comes up into my hand. I don't need a stone for it."

Dee looked at her for as long as it took to breathe in.

"Slowly," she said. "And off it when I say. And you'll not bend."

"I'll not bend."

So Callie lifted, on her knees by the door, with her back straight and her arm across her side and her right palm flat on the grey. Jab lifted by the carrier, crouched, with his palm an inch off first every time, feeling for the cold, and then down on it. He took only where it was cold, and moved his hand wherever it was warm. Toren lifted one-handed at the edge of the slope by the boxes, slowly, at his own rate, which was the slowest in the yard.

And Dee stood by the cabinets with the glass, and the counter on her shoulder, and called the turns.

---

It came up cold.

It came up into Toren's right palm slow and heavy and cold, the way it had on the east track when Rook knelt beside him and said *don't haul*. There was nothing in it. There was nobody in it to want anything, and it did not want to go anywhere now. It lay in his palm and was ash.

He held it high. He took it to the cabinet top one-handed and tipped a jar against his palm, and it went down out of him into the glass, and lay there, grey.

He went back.

He could not do anything fast. He had never been able to. He crouched at the edge of the slope with his left arm against his chest and his right palm flat and let it come at the rate it came, and the rate it came for him was slow. Across the room Jab filled a jar while he filled a quarter of one. By the door Callie filled half a jar on her knees with her face grey and set, and did not bend.

"Five," said Dee. "The counter's a hundred and ten. It's coming down. It's settling."

He watched the grey settle. It had been in the air when the lifter fell, a little, and the counter had been at a hundred and forty. There was no wind under the hill. It came down out of the air onto the floor and lay there with the rest.

"Ten," said Dee.

Jab stoppered a jar and started another. "One," he said. "Cold. All of it."

"Twelve."

Callie's hand stopped.

"This one's got grit in it," she said, looking at her own palm. "I can see it. It's not like the plain. It came up with the rest."

"Put it in your second," said Dee. "Apart. We'll grade it at the river."

"Fifteen."

Jab stoppered the second. Callie stoppered her first and set her second going. Toren had half a jar, near enough, and his palm ached the way Callie had said it ached on the east track, from the inside.

"Sixteen," said Dee. "The box."

---

He had not forgotten the box. He had been lifting round it.

Jab had lifted the grey off the lid. Toren put his good hand on the lid and lifted it out of its groove, and the first box was there, in the lead bottom, where it had landed yesterday with a sound like a door in another house.

The second one was on the floor at the edge of the heap of boxes. It had been lying there with a strand of grey across its top like a hair across a cheek. The strand was a line of grey on the steel now. It was not going anywhere.

Callie could not do the tongs. She knew it before Toren said it. She looked at them where they lay by the boxes, where she had dropped them yesterday, a yard and a half of iron gone grey to the rings. She looked at her own arms across her side.

"You," she said to Jab.

Jab took the tongs.

He had never used them. He got the jaws round the box that said a hundred and six on its sides, and closed the rings, and the jaws bit. He did not lift. He dragged it along the floor through the grey to the side of the carrier.

Toren set a strut.

He did not have much to set it with. He had a hair above his floor. He put the foot of it on the concrete at a slant up to the lip of the carrier, and it came up out of the floor thin and pale, and he was soft behind it.

"Set," he said. "Walk it up."

Jab walked it up. The box slid up the pale line of the strut to the lip and tipped and went over and landed on the lead beside the first one.

"Two," said Toren.

He let the strut go. He went down to look and did not like what he saw, and did not say it.

"And the third?" said Dee.

Toren looked at the heap of boxes.

The third one was there. He could see it, half under two others, deep in, where Callie had read it yesterday at a hundred and forty with its slide rusted halfway across its window. It was talking to the room. It had been talking to the room for twenty years.

There might be others. There were hundreds of boxes on the floor. Callie had read a dozen yesterday and found three. There might be a live one with a shut slide in the next dozen, or the next. It would take her a quarter of an hour on her knees with the counter at a hand. She could not do it. Nobody else could read it the way she did.

"Seventeen," said Dee.

"Two," said Toren. "Not three. The third's open. We'd be carrying what the lead's meant to stop." He put the lid in its groove. It went in with a soft heavy sound. "Two."

"Two," said Dee. "Written."

---

The vent jars stayed.

There were three of them in the canvas bag on the cabinet, stoppered, heavy, full of what Jab had taken off the lifter at every three. Dee held the counter to the bag and did not say the number out loud. She took the chalk out of her pocket, Pell's chalk, and set the bag on the floor at the foot of the racks, and drew a cross on the concrete beside it.

"It stays under the hill," she said. "Where it came from."

"Nineteen," she said. "Twenty. Out."

---

They went out.

The sledge went out through the door over the threshold, with the carrier on it and two live boxes in the carrier and the lid in its groove. Toren had the front rope. Jab had it behind him, with the frame on his back. Five jars of the lifter in it, three full and two not, and the fence's last whole jar, and the fourth jar under the line with the rest of what was in it. Callie walked beside the sledge with the hook for a stick and her arm across her side and did not pull. Dee came last with the glass.

The corridor was grey. The big rope was a bank of grey along the foot of the left-hand wall, the shape of a rope, lying there. The tray on the right-hand wall was full of grey, and not moving. They went along it and nothing moved in it.

At the foot of the stair Toren stopped and looked at the ramp.

It went up. Thirty steps' worth of smooth concrete, to the landing, and twenty more. The iron ring was set in the wall at the landing.

"On a slope, up or down," he said, "there's a rope round something that doesn't move."

"And nobody below it," said Callie.

They took it up the ramp on the front rope through the ring at the landing. Toren and Jab pulled. Callie stood at the top of the first flight with the end of the back rope round her waist, not pulling, holding, so that if the front rope went, the sledge would not go down onto anybody. Dee stood beside her.

It came up the ramp a pace at a time.

At the landing it had to stop against the turn of the wall while they moved the rope to the ring at the top. Toren set a strut under the back of the board where it stood on the slope, so it would not slide while the rope was off.

It held for four breaths. He felt it take the last of him above his floor.

"I'm at my floor," he said. "I'm stopping."

He let it go. The rope was on the top ring. The board did not move.

"Pull," said Jab.

They pulled it up the second flight, and it came over the top step into the hall of the long building, into the light from the door.

---

It was the middle of the afternoon. The sun was going down over the drum.

At the top of the road Callie stopped. She could hardly stand straight. She turned round anyway and looked up, because she had said she would, every time, going in and coming out.

Toren took the paper out.

She counted. He watched her lips. Ten. Three.

Ten. Four.

She stopped.

"It's not," she said.

He looked up.

The edge of the dark over the drum thickened. He counted without meaning to. Eight. And it thinned. And it thickened again, at once, before it had thinned for three. And thinned. And thickened for a long time, twelve, fourteen, and thinned for five.

"It's lost the count," said Callie.

She watched it for a long time. Nothing came out of the bottom of it while she watched. Then something did, a long grey veil, at no count at all, in the middle of a thickening, and went down behind the broken rim of the drum.

"One," she said. "But not at the three. There isn't a three."

Toren wrote it. *Coming out, after. The sky's not ten and three. It's lost the count. 1 fall, not at a three.*

He looked at the words for a while.

"It's still there," said Jab, beside him. He was looking up at the dark over the drum. "The sky. That's still there."

"It was never the lifter," said Dee.

"No," said Jab. "The lifter was keeping time to it. Or it was keeping time to the lifter." He looked at the white place on his palm. "I don't know which."

---

The bridge had poles on it.

Toren saw them from the gate. Two long poles of grey wrack wood, the two they had used to slide the carrier off the cart onto the tailboard. They lay along the bridge on the far end, one on the outside plank on each side, over the crack in the middle one. They lay long ways, reaching from the cross-beam before the crack to the cross-beam after it, so that anything on them would sit on the beams and not on the rotten plank.

Rook was lying at the foot of the first post on the near side.

He was not sitting. He was lying on his side on the gravel with his coat half off and his face grey and running wet, and his eyes were open, watching the gate.

Toren brought the gate round behind them and twisted the green wire back through the chain. He did it one-handed. He left the ends so they would come.

They brought the sledge across the bridge on the poles. The board slid along them, over the crack, and the middle plank took nothing.

At the near end Dee knelt on the gravel beside Rook.

"You were sitting," she said.

"I was sitting," said Rook. "I've been looking at that plank all day. I knew what he'd have left for it, coming out." He did not look at Toren. "I didn't light anything. I dragged two poles along a bridge on my knees. That's not working. That's furniture."

"You're off," said Dee.

"I'm off," said Rook. "I'm very off." He shut his eyes. "Count them."

---

Pell had them tip the cart.

He told them from the boards, sitting with his back to the rack and his leg out. Lift the shafts, all of you that can, till the tail comes down. It's a cart, not a house. When the shafts go up, the tail goes down, and the tailboard's gone anyway, and the bed's a ramp to the ground.

They lifted the shafts, Jab and Toren and Dee, and the tail of the cart came down onto the gravel. They pulled the sledge up the bed of the cart on the front rope, with Callie holding the back rope and not pulling. When it was up behind the home jars, they let the shafts down slowly, and the cart came level, and the carrier sat on its board on the bed of the cart with two live boxes in it.

"There," said Pell. "Lead's not for backs."

---

Coat off. Shake it. Wash at the river below the pump house, the hands and the face and the hair, before you eat.

They did it. Callie did it on her knees at the edge of the water with one hand, and Dee washed her hair for her, slowly, and Callie let her.

Then Toren stood at the pump house wall with the signed paper against the stone.

"Dee," he said. "Jab. Callie."

He looked at Rook, on the canvas where they had carried him, with his eyes open. At Pell on the cart, with his leg out and his pencil. At the mare, tied to the wheel with her rope halter, eating.

"Four in," he said. "Six. And a horse."

He wrote it.

*48th. The pump house. In: 4. The lifter's stopped. T. put the point in, at the open, with J. on the push and C. in the door. Under the line: 2 turns, T. and D. W.'s call. 2 boxes in the carrier, in lead, on the cart. The third left (open). C.'s ribs cracked (left, the same). D. W. at her floor (once). T. at his. The sky's lost the count.*

He looked at it for a while.

Under it, in the same hand, he wrote: *Everybody back.*

---

## CHAPTER — books/book-02-meridian/manuscript/chapter-40.md

# Chapter 40 — Callie: Graded

Jab drew them at the river, all of them, alone.

There was nobody else to do it. Miss Wren was at her floor. Rook was off, and hot, and lying on the canvas with his eyes open. So Jab stood on the gravel below the pump house in the last of the sun with a jar of the lifter's ash against his left palm and his right hand flat on one chest after another, and drew.

Callie held the counter for him. She held it with her right hand, because her left arm would not go out from her side without the ribs saying so. She held it at arm's length to each chest in turn and said the number, and Jab drew until she said a smaller one.

"Ninety," she said, at Toren. And later, "Twenty." And later, "Twelve. Off."

He did Toren. He did Callie, with his palm flat on the inside of her wrist, because the ribs were where the rest of it would have been. He did Miss Wren.

He did Miss Wren longest.

She stood in front of him with her hands at her sides and let him. Callie watched her face while he did it. It was white, and it was not steady now. It had been steady all morning under the hill, over the jar and the glass and Jab's back, nineteen times. It had stopped being steady on the bridge.

"A hundred and ten," said Callie, at Miss Wren's chest. "Ten breaths."

Jab did not say anything. He drew. He held it high and vented it into the gravel downstream of the pump house, away from the water, the road's and not the river's. He went back on.

"Sixty," said Callie, after a long time. "Thirty. Fourteen."

"Off," said Miss Wren. Her voice was thin. "That's enough. That's the room."

Jab came off.

Then he stood with his hands held out a little from his sides, the way he had stood in the cut at the wash. Nobody took them. There was nobody to take them.

"I'll vent," he said. "Long."

He went down the gravel to the far end, past the stone with the chalk cross on it and the two hot jars from yesterday standing beside it, and knelt, and put both palms flat on the stones there. He stayed there while the sun went down behind the hill.

---

She graded the jars at the fire.

Nobody asked her to. She took the counter and went to the frame where it stood against the pump house wall on its two flat stones, and she held the counter to each jar in the rack at a hand's width, one at a time, and waited ten breaths for each. And said it out loud, because that was what you did with a number.

"The fence's last whole one," she said. "Eight. The room. It's clean. Eight in it." She moved along. "The fence's fourth. The one under the line. Eight. Clean. Five in it."

She weighed it on her palm. Five, near enough.

Then the lifter's.

"Jab's first," she said. "Nine. Clean. Full. Eight." She moved along. "Jab's second. Eight. Clean. Six in it. He's drawn two out of it at the river."

"Mine," said Toren, from the fire.

"Yours is empty," said Callie. "It went on the four of us first. It was only a half." She moved along. "Mine. The first." She held the counter to it for ten breaths and then for ten more, because she wanted to be sure. "Ten. It's clean. Full. Eight."

She came to the last one.

"My second," she said. "Nineteen."

She held it up to the fire and looked through the glass at the grey in the bottom. It was grey. But there were darker grains in it, fine, like pepper in flour, all through.

"It's dirty," she said. "There's grit in it. The hill. It came up with the rest. I felt it come and I didn't know it was coming." She put it in the rack apart from the others, in the socket at the end where jar B had been, with the broken glass taken out of it now. "Four in it. Dirty. It'll burn wrong."

"It'll burn wrong," said Miss Wren, from the canvas by Rook. "It'll not go on anybody's back unless there's nothing else. Write it dirty."

Toren wrote it.

Callie stood by the frame and said the whole of it. She said it the way her mother said the store book, flat, for somebody else. The way she had said it every halt on the old line and every night since.

"Inside," she said. "The fence's, thirteen. Five in the fourth, eight in the last. The lifter's, twenty-two, clean. Four, dirty. Thirty-five clean. Four dirty." She stopped. "Home, twenty-four. On the cart. Not touched. The road jar's empty. The vent jars are under the hill, and two by the stone."

"Say what we went in with," said Toren.

"Forty," said Callie. "Clean. On the forty-seventh."

Nobody said anything for a while.

"Thirty-five and four," said Pell, from the cart. "Against forty." He was quiet. "And two boxes."

"And three under the line for two turns," said Miss Wren. "And my floor. And his." She nodded at Toren. "And her ribs. And his hand." She did not look toward the far end of the gravel, where Jab was still kneeling. "And the rest of it on the floor of that room."

"How much is on the floor of that room?" said Pell.

Callie thought about it. She thought about the long slope of grey from the carrier to the threshold, knee-deep in the middle. The bank of it along the corridor wall, the shape of a rope. The tray full of it, the whole length of the corridor into the dark. The stair.

"More than we lifted," she said. "Four times. Five." She shook her head. "I don't know. It's not a number. It's more than we could lift in a day, with nobody hurt."

"Then write that," said Toren. "*More than we could lift.*"

---

"What was it?" said Pell.

He said it to Rook. He said it the way he asked the weighbridge about a jar, not unkindly. Rook was lying on his side on the canvas with his face to the fire and his eyes half shut.

"The Director'll want a word for it," said Pell. "On the paper. They grade them. A Flask, or a Barrel. She'll want to know which." He looked across the river at the hill in the dusk. "It came along a rope and had its hand in Jab. It mended everything anybody did to it every thirteen breaths. It had the whole hill." He looked at Rook. "That's a Barrel, surely."

Rook opened his eyes.

"A Barrel's a thing on its own," he said.

He said it slowly, because he was hot and his mouth was dry. Miss Wren held the cup for him and he drank and went on.

"A Barrel comes out of a tear all by itself and it's big on its own account. What's in it is its own." He looked at the fire. "That wasn't big. That was a Flask. Maybe. A Flask with its hand in everybody's pocket." He shut his eyes again. "All that grey on the floor wasn't it. It was everybody else's. The husks'. The hounds' at the wash. The jar that broke on the rail. Twenty years of whatever came out of that sky and fell on the hill and lay there. It went and got it all, and put it on, like a coat that's too big." He was quiet. "Write it a Flask. Write the rest as what it had."

Toren wrote it. Callie watched him do it. *Its own: a Flask (R.). What it had: more than we could lift.*

"You'd know," said Pell.

"I'd know," said Rook. "It had my job."

---

Miss Wren was sick in the night.

Callie heard it before she saw it. She was on the first watch with Pell, because Pell was awake and Callie could not lie down without the ribs, and she was standing by the cart wheel in the dark with the hook for a stick. She heard somebody get up off the gravel by the wall, fast, and go down toward the river, and be sick. It went on for a long time.

Jab was already there. He had not been asleep either. He went down the gravel to her in the dark, and Callie saw the two of them against the grey of the water, Miss Wren on her knees with her hand on a stone and Jab crouched beside her.

"It's the room," said Miss Wren, when she could. Her voice carried along the water. "It's twenty turns in that room after, at a hundred and thirty, with nobody drawing me. It's the room coming out." She was sick again. "It'll be the room for a day."

"Hold still," said Jab.

"I'm holding still."

"You're not. You're shaking."

"I'm allowed to shake," said Miss Wren. "I'm not allowed to be drawn at my floor. There's nobody to draw you after."

"There's nobody to draw me now," said Jab. "You're busy. I can feel it from here. Hold still."

Callie did not hear what Miss Wren said to that. She saw her sit back on her heels on the stones. She saw Jab put his palm flat on her back, between the shoulders, and his other hand on the jar he had brought, and bend his head.

She watched them for a long time. Then she looked away, because it was not hers to watch.

---

Pell was counting the sky.

He sat on the cart with his back to the rack and his leg out, the way he had sat all three days, with the sheet on his knee that he could not see to mark in the dark. He was looking across the river at the hill. Over its flat top there were no stars. Round the edge of the place with no stars there was the light the colour of the bottom of a pond.

"It's not ten and three," said Pell.

"I know. We saw it at the top of the road."

"I've been counting it since dusk." He did not take his eyes off it. "It's not anything. Eight, and five. Then twelve and two. Then fourteen and nothing, and it goes again. It's thickening and thinning, the same as it was. But there's no count to it. It's like a man breathing in his sleep who's having a bad dream." He was quiet. "It had a count for three days. Every one. I'd have put my name to it."

"It had the lifter," said Callie.

"Or the lifter had it," said Pell. "Jab doesn't know which. I don't. I'd like to know which." He moved his leg on the boards and made a small sound. "Write it down. Tomorrow. That woman'll want it more than the boxes, I think. Or as much."

"She'll want the boxes."

"She'll want the boxes," Pell agreed. "And she'll want to know why a thing that counted to thirteen for three days stopped counting the afternoon somebody put a point in a heap of grey under it." He looked at Callie in the dark. "I wouldn't like to be the one who has to decide what to do about that. With a Sag held and a machine that can see."

Callie looked at the sky over the hill. It thickened for a long time, and thinned, and thickened at once.

She did not count it. It had nothing to count.

---

She thought about the door.

She had not let herself think about it all afternoon. There had been the stair, and the road, and the bridge, and the river, and the jars. Now there was only the dark and Pell and the cart wheel, and she thought about it.

She had held it. She had stood in it with her back to the room for twenty turns and more, with the stone going out of her into a door and a bit, and a husk pushed against the pale a foot from her face. She had never once seen what was happening behind her. She had heard it. She had heard the sack hit the wall. She had heard Toren say *holding* with his teeth together, and Jab say *busy*, and Miss Wren say *off*, over and over, and the glass going over. She had heard the line said. She had heard *two turns*, and said *I've got two turns, I've not got three*, and she had not known, saying it, whether two would be enough. She had not been able to do anything about it if it was not.

She had looked round once. Rook had said she would.

She had turned her head and seen Jab on the floor with his hand out, and Miss Wren behind him with the jar, and the room behind that, grey. And Jab had looked at her and said *busy*. Just that. The word he said to tell you he was working and how long you had to hold.

And she had turned her head back and held.

She had not carried them. That was what she understood, standing by the cart wheel in the dark with her arm across her side. She had carried people all her life. She had carried her mother across four hundred miles in her head, every breath, every step, counting her. She had held a wagon on a hook with her mother in it. On the lip at the wash she had held a lee off her own stone over two bodies and taken a third in and out of it with her eyes. She had thought that was what it was for. Holding people.

Under the hill she had held a door. She had held the rest of the world off three people so they could do the thing only they could do, where she could not see them do it. She had not been holding them. She had been holding the edge of them. And they had held the rest.

*Stop holding so hard. Let what's been built hold.*

She had thought, when her mother said it, that it meant letting go. It did not mean letting go. It meant choosing what to hold, and holding that, all of it, and trusting the people on the other side of it to hold their own.

She had not known that it would feel like this. It did not feel like forgetting. It felt like the ache in the palm after lifting, when you have put a thing down that was worth carrying.

---

In the morning Miss Wren measured them.

She did it sitting on a stone by the cold fire, because she was not inside a fence and she was allowed, and because her legs would not have done it standing. Her face was grey and there were marks under her eyes like bruises. But her hands were steady again. Callie watched them go to each wrist in turn.

"Your own's back," she said to Toren. "A turn. Not two. It'll be two by tonight if you do nothing."

"I'll do nothing."

"You'll carry a wheel," said Miss Wren. "But not today." She did Callie. "Your own's back. Half. You slept?"

"No."

"You slept an hour," said Miss Wren. "Jab said. Your own's half." She put her palm flat against Callie's side, not pressing, over the coat. "And that's cracked. Two, I think. The same two." She took her hand away. "I'll strap it. You'll not lift anything heavier than a counter for a week. You'll not lean on that hook."

"I'll lean on it to walk."

"You'll lean on it to walk," Miss Wren agreed. "That's what it's for this week."

She did Jab. She held his palms in both of hers, turned up. The white place on the heel of the right one was red round its edges now, and shiny, and there was a small blister at one side of it.

"They're warm," she said.

"I vented half the night."

"I know you did. I watched you. I was being sick, and I watched you." She put her palms flat on his, palm to palm, and Callie saw her find it. She drew. It was the watch taking it off the one who had worked, the way she had done it in the cut at the wash. Her face went quiet while she did it. "There," she said. "Cool."

"You're at your floor," said Jab.

"I'm a finger over it," said Miss Wren. "I slept too. After you'd finished with me." She let go of his hands. "That's enough for a watch. It's not enough for anything else. Not today."

She did her own, last, with her two fingers on the inside of her crooked wrist.

"A finger," she said. "Over." She wrote it.

---

Rook was holding.

That was Miss Wren's word for it, and she said it the way she had said it about the post on the arch. The red round the stitches had not gone further. It had not come in. He was hot, but not as hot as the first morning, and he drank the third of Sowerby's papers in boiled water and made the face.

"One left," said Miss Wren.

"Keep it," said Rook. "For whoever's worse."

"You're the worst."

"Then keep it for when I'm worse than this," said Rook. He looked at her across the cup. "You were sick."

"I was the watch," said Miss Wren. "Nobody watched me." She took the cup. "I'll not do it that way again."

"Yes you will," said Rook. "If it's the same thing again, you'll do it the same way." He shut his eyes. "I'd like it written down that I said so."

"I'll write that you said so," said Miss Wren. "I'll not write that you were right."

He laughed. It was the low careful laugh, the way a man laughs with a hole in his back. Callie saw Miss Wren's mouth move at the corner, once, and stop.

---

They decided at the cart.

Pell had the atlas open on his knee. Toren had the paper. Everybody else stood, or sat on what they were allowed to sit on, round the tail of the cart where the carrier sat on its board behind the home jars with two live boxes in it and the lid in its groove.

"Say the clock," said Toren.

"The forty-ninth," said Pell. "Today. The cycle's the fifty-fourth. Five days." He put his finger on the atlas. "It was six days from the gate to here, with the siding. With the road whole."

"It isn't whole," said Callie.

"It isn't whole," said Pell. "The crown's gone into the wash, and the post, and the arch. There's a gap in the embankment four paces wide with the wash running through the bottom of it and the drifts heaped on both sides." He looked at the three of them. "North round the head of the drifts, toward the hill. Or south through the marsh to the river and down this bank. Two days more, either way, and there's no bed for the cart on either. That's what I said on the forty-sixth. It's still true."

"That's the fifty-seventh," said Toren. "Home."

"Or later," said Pell. "With me on the cart and not walking."

"And the other thing?" said Toren. "What you said at the fire. That it'll take people across the bottom on foot."

"On foot," said Pell. "Through the drifts. Quick, and wash after. It won't take the cart." He looked at the cart under him, and the carrier on it, and the three home jars. "Nothing takes a cart across that."

He was quiet for a long time.

"Nothing takes a cart across that," he said again, slowly. He put his hand flat on the boards of the cart bed. "A cart's not a thing. It's a lot of things. Two wheels. An axle. A bed. Two shafts. Pins." He looked up. "A cart in pieces goes across in pieces."

Nobody said anything.

"The wheels roll," said Pell. "You roll them. Down one side of the gap and up the other. They're as high as her shoulder." He nodded at Callie. "The axle's two men. The bed's four, or two and a horse with a rope on it. The shafts are one each. The carrier's on its board, and a board slides on a drift. It's flour." He looked at Toren. "And there's a bank on each side of the gap, loose. And you set steps on a loose bank at the wash. On stones."

"I set them on my own," said Toren. "The last of it."

"You'll have a day's worth by tomorrow," said Pell. "Miss Wren says two turns by tonight. And there's a jar of that thing's ash to settle, if it's clean."

"And I'm a bed and two poles and a bit of canvas," said Pell. "Two of you. I'll not say anything about the camber."

---

"Price it," said Toren.

He said it to Callie.

She stood by the tail of the cart with her arm across her side and the counter in her other hand, and she priced it. She did it out loud, because that was the rule, and because saying it was how she found out whether it was true.

"The drifts were eighty, ninety at the foot of the bank," she said. "In the wind. With no wind, lying still, they'll be less. Sixty, seventy. Where the crown went in, where it's turned over, more. A hundred." She looked east, toward the ridge and the cut and the wash behind it, a mile off. "An hour. For all of it. Down, across, up, the pieces, and Pell, and the horse, twice over for the wheels. An hour at eighty. All of us, and the horse."

"How much?" said Miss Wren.

"Ten," said Callie. "Maybe twelve. For the drawing after, in the cut on the far side. And the settling for Toren's steps." She looked at the frame against the wall. "Out of the lifter's."

"Out of the lifter's," said Toren.

"Out of the lifter's," said Callie. "It's got twenty and a half clean. A Handful and a half went on Miss Wren in the night." She did not look at Miss Wren. "It'd have ten and a half left, and the fence's thirteen, and the dirty four. And home, twenty-four, not touched." She stopped. "And we'd be on the old line east of the gap by the fiftieth. With the cart back together. And it's five days from there, on the road we know. With the siding." She did the last sum. "The fifty-fifth. Home."

"Not the fifty-fourth," said Toren.

"Not the fifty-fourth," said Callie. "Nothing's the fifty-fourth. The fifty-fourth was gone before we crossed the bridge."

Toren looked at the paper for a long time.

"It's the fifty-fifth, or the fifty-seventh," he said. "Or later." He looked up. "That's two days of Sowerby dosing her off her blood, badly. And whatever the fifty-fourth does, with a man guessing." He did not say *Gran*. "And ten Handfuls of that thing's ash, and an hour in the drifts, and Callie's ribs, and Dee a finger over her floor."

"Say which," said Miss Wren.

"The wash," said Toren. "In pieces. Tomorrow, first light, if the wind's down. Not today. Nobody carries a wheel through a drift at their floor."

"Not today," said Miss Wren, and wrote it. "And if the wind's up?"

"Then we sit at the cut and wait for it to drop," said Toren. "And I'll write what it costs."

Pell shut the atlas.

"Then today we go to the cut," he said. "It's a mile. The cart'll do that whole. It's this side of the gap." He looked at Duchess, tied to the wheel with her rope halter, eating. "She'll pull it to the cut. And tomorrow she'll walk through a drift, because somebody she knows is walking in front of her. And then she'll hate every one of us for a week."

"She'll be right," said Rook, from the canvas.

"She's always right," said Pell. "She's a horse."

---

They loaded the cart.

The frame went on the cart this time, in the rack behind the driver's bench beside the home jars, because Jab's palm was blistered and Miss Wren said he would not carry eight jars a mile on a blister. Rook went on the cart on the canvas, on his side, with his back off the boards. Pell stayed where he was. The carrier sat behind them on its board with the lid in its groove.

Toren went round the cart once, the whole of it, and counted what was on it. Then he counted them.

"Pell," he said. "Dee. Rook." He stopped on Rook the way he always did, longest. "Jab. Callie." He looked at the mare. "Six. And a horse."

He wrote it on the back of the signed paper, standing at the pump house wall.

*49th. The pump house. Out: 6. To the cut. The wash tomorrow, in pieces (Pell's). Home the 55th, if the wind's down.*

He looked at it.

"Dessa left six lines," he said. "At the foot of the page. For *back*."

"I know," said Callie.

"I'll not write in them yet," said Toren. He folded the paper and put it in his coat, beside the plate.

---

Callie went out in front.

She did it without being asked, the way she had done it on the first morning on the old north road. She went out twenty paces in front of Duchess with the hook for a stick and her left arm strapped across her side under her coat. She began to read the road for the wheels.

It was the road they had come on, two days ago, at dusk. The old line going up from the river on its long bare slope to the ridge, and the cut through the ridge, and the wash behind it. She knew it. She read it anyway. She read it for the wheels. The loose stones on the bed. The place where the frost had heaved it. The mark where the cart had come down it on the forty-sixth with Pell on it and his foot on a coil of rope.

At the top of the slope, at the mouth of the cut, she stopped and turned round.

She looked back once. Everybody does.

The river lay low and dark in its stony bed, with the pump house on its near bank, roofless, and the two broken pipes going into the water. The footbridge went across it on its posts, with two grey poles of wrack wood still lying along it over the crack. At the far end, the gate, with a twist of green wire through its chain, left so it would come.

And beyond the gate the yard, and the road going up the hill in its long Z, and the long building standing along the rim with its windows black. And the drum behind it with its broken top. And over the whole of the top of the hill, the sky.

The edge of it thickened. She did not count it. It thinned, and thickened at once, and thinned for a long time. It had no count. It was a thing breathing in its sleep, having a bad dream.

Under it, in a room under a hill, there was more ash lying on a floor than she could have lifted in a week. There were hundreds of boxes, and a third live one talking to the room, and three stoppered jars with a chalk cross beside them. She would put the number of every one of them on the Director's table.

In the cart behind Duchess there were two.

It was the forty-ninth day. At home it would be the ninth hour, near enough. Her mother would have walked to the ward for her pills at the eighth hour, with Katori on one side of her and Ines on the other, not holding. Senna would be on the fourth bed on the right, with five days to the narrow bed and the machine that sang flat. Tonk would be in the fifth, with a cord round his fist. They were all breathing without her. They had been breathing without her for eight days, and nothing had happened to any of them because she was not counting.

She did not know what would happen on the fifty-fourth. She did not know if the wind would be down tomorrow.

She knew what was in the cart, and what was in the frame, graded. She knew who was on the cart and who was walking. She knew the road to the cut, and she knew the wash.

She turned round and went on into the cut, twenty paces in front of the horse, and read the road for the wheels.

Behind her, at the back, she heard Toren counting them.

---

## FORMULA ALIGNMENT — EDITORIAL, NOT BLIND

# O'Connor 1.3.0 — numerical author formula

# Applying the owner's numerical craft formula

The owner explicitly selected
`/Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md` to govern this book.
Use its craft instructions more or less exactly, including the numbers. These
are active design targets for the planning model and Opus/Fable author, not merely
editorial observations. The compiler embeds sections 2–6 and 8–9 verbatim below
these instructions and records the source hash so runs can be compared.

Let the sentence-length distribution, short/long sentence shares, paragraph
rhythm and Flesch reading targets influence the actual prose. Let the POV shares,
front-loaded progression vocabulary and distributed character-development rates
influence how the book is built. Preserve section 8's secondary/use-to-taste
status; do not turn its explicitly excluded filler/repetition tics into quotas.

Write original scenes in 6–8-chapter movements. The formula constrains the intended
shape and cadence; the author still chooses conversations, tactics, discoveries
and how events unfold. Do not reduce this brief to “clear writing with progression.”
The owner names the cast. No source novel quotations, character substitutions or
scene-by-scene replication belong in the manuscript.

Aim at the supplied distributions across a movement and the cumulative book.
Book-third teaching proportions and book-level POV proportions need not be met
independently by each chapter. Do not stop after each sentence or chapter to run a
gate. Write the full movement, then compare target versus observed results in the
existing editorial pass and make a focused same-author repair for material drift.
Do not silently broaden tolerances or drop inconvenient targets.

Keep the source's own caveats: pause-derived paragraph/scene measures are
directional proxies, and development counts are heuristic. Their adoption as
design targets does not establish that they accurately measure the printed book.
The full source-book audit remains unfinished. Keep source values such as ~1.7x
and ~950 words unchanged; the known arithmetic/denominator questions belong in
the audit, not a silent rewrite of the owner's chosen prompt. If competing targets
cannot be reconciled, report the conflict rather than fabricating a passing score.

This prompt guides output; it does not train model weights or guarantee exact
statistical compliance. Compliance must be measured after prose exists.

Source: /Users/drive/penname/research/ironprince-craft-formula.md
Source SHA-256: 9f97e2f225c0bcf61d2e922719153fcf00e3b029f7b1552f213888d33130c0b8

The following craft sections (2–6, 8–9) are included verbatim. Sections 1 and 7's legal discussion is not drafting guidance. Use original names, world, systems, events and phrasing; the owner names the cast.

## 2. Sentence rhythm

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Mean sentence length | 14.6 words |
| Median sentence length | 11 words |
| Spread (population stdev) | ~26 words — wide, meaning short declaratives sit right next to long compound-complex sentences, not clustered near the mean |
| Share of very short sentences (≤5 words) | 27.7% |
| Share of very long sentences (≥40 words) | 3.3% |

**Read:** roughly 1 sentence in 4 is a hard, short beat (often a single clause landing a
punch after a longer setup) — this is the "short sentence for impact" technique, not
uniform mid-length prose. Target this ratio, not just the mean; a manuscript that hits mean
14.6 but has no short-sentence spikes will not read the same.

## 3. Paragraph and scene rhythm (ASR-inferred proxy)

Whisper transcripts carry no typographic paragraph marks — this section is inferred from
pause gaps between narrated speech segments, not a count of the print manuscript's actual
paragraph breaks. Treat as directional, not exact.

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
| Inferred paragraph length (pause ≥0.6s) | median 18 words, mean 26.8 words — short, frequent beats |
| Inferred scene/section length (pause ≥1.6s) | mean ~950 words between scene-level breaks, ~8.7 scene breaks per 10k words |

**Read:** short paragraphs are the default texture, with scenes running roughly 900–1000
words before a beat change (POV settle, location change, or time-skip). Avoid long
unbroken expository blocks; break early and often.

## 4. Progression-mechanic ("change frequency") callout rate

| Metric | Target |
|---|---|
